</tr><tr><td class="sidebar-navbar"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1129693374"/><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1063604349">.mw-parser-output .navbar{display:inline;font-size:88%;font-weight:normal}.mw-parser-output .navbar-collapse{float:left;text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .navbar-boxtext{word-spacing:0}.mw-parser-output .navbar ul{display:inline-block;white-space:nowrap;line-height:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::before{margin-right:-0.125em;content:"[ "}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::after{margin-left:-0.125em;content:" ]"}.mw-parser-output .navbar li{word-spacing:-0.125em}.mw-parser-output .navbar a>span,.mw-parser-output .navbar a>abbr{text-decoration:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-mini abbr{font-variant:small-caps;border-bottom:none;text-decoration:none;cursor:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-full{font-size:114%;margin:0 7em}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-mini{font-size:114%;margin:0 4em}</style><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:Eastern_Orthodox_Church_sidebar" title="Template:Eastern Orthodox Church sidebar"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:Eastern_Orthodox_Church_sidebar" title="Template talk:Eastern Orthodox Church sidebar"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Template:Eastern_Orthodox_Church_sidebar&amp;action=edit"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Basil of Caesarea</b>, also called <b>Saint Basil the Great</b> (<a href="/wiki/Koin%C4%93_Greek_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Koinē Greek language">Koinē Greek</a>: <span lang="grc">Ἅγιος Βασίλειος ὁ Μέγας</span>, <i>Hágios Basíleios ho Mégas</i>; <a href="/wiki/Coptic_language" title="Coptic language">Coptic</a>: <span lang="cop">Ⲡⲓⲁⲅⲓⲟⲥ Ⲃⲁⲥⲓⲗⲓⲟⲥ</span>; 330<sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> – 1 or 2 January 379), was a bishop of <a href="/wiki/Caesarea_Mazaca" class="mw-redirect" title="Caesarea Mazaca">Caesarea Mazaca</a> in <a href="/wiki/Cappadocia_(Roman_province)" title="Cappadocia (Roman province)">Cappadocia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Asia_Minor" class="mw-redirect" title="Asia Minor">Asia Minor</a> (modern-day Turkey). He was an influential theologian who supported the <a href="/wiki/Nicene_Creed" title="Nicene Creed">Nicene Creed</a> and opposed the <a href="/wiki/Heresy" title="Heresy">heresies</a> of the early Christian church, fighting against both <a href="/wiki/Arianism" title="Arianism">Arianism</a> and the followers of <a href="/wiki/Apollinaris_of_Laodicea" title="Apollinaris of Laodicea">Apollinaris of Laodicea</a>. His ability to balance his theological convictions with his political connections made Basil a powerful advocate for the Nicene position.
</p><p>In addition to his work as a theologian, Basil was known for his care of the poor and underprivileged. Basil established guidelines for monastic life which focus on community life, liturgical prayer, and manual labor. Together with <a href="/wiki/Pachomius" class="mw-redirect" title="Pachomius">Pachomius</a>, he is remembered as a father of <a href="/wiki/Cenobitic_monasticism" title="Cenobitic monasticism">communal monasticism</a> in Eastern Christianity. He is considered a <a href="/wiki/Saint" title="Saint">saint</a> by the traditions of both <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Christianity" title="Eastern Christianity">Eastern</a> and <a href="/wiki/Western_Christianity" title="Western Christianity">Western Christianity</a>.
</p><p>Basil, together with his brother <a href="/wiki/Gregory_of_Nyssa" title="Gregory of Nyssa">Gregory of Nyssa</a> and his friend <a href="/wiki/Gregory_of_Nazianzus" title="Gregory of Nazianzus">Gregory of Nazianzus</a>, are collectively referred to as the <a href="/wiki/Cappadocian_Fathers" title="Cappadocian Fathers">Cappadocian Fathers</a>. The <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church" title="Eastern Orthodox Church">Eastern Orthodox Church</a> and <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Catholic_Churches" title="Eastern Catholic Churches">Eastern Catholic Churches</a> have given him, together with Gregory of Nazianzus and <a href="/wiki/John_Chrysostom" title="John Chrysostom">John Chrysostom</a>, the title of <a href="/wiki/Three_Holy_Hierarchs" title="Three Holy Hierarchs">Great Hierarch</a>. He is recognized as a <a href="/wiki/Doctor_of_the_Church" title="Doctor of the Church">Doctor of the Church</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Church" class="mw-redirect" title="Roman Catholic Church">Roman Catholic Church</a>. He is sometimes referred to by the epithet <i>Ouranophantor</i> (Greek: <span title="Ancient Greek (to 1453)-language text"><span lang="grc">Οὐρανοφάντωρ</span></span>), "revealer of heavenly mysteries".<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Life">Life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Basil_of_Caesarea&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life_and_education">Early life and education</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Basil_of_Caesarea&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Early life and education">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Basil was born into the wealthy <a href="/wiki/Cappadocian_Greeks" title="Cappadocian Greeks">Cappadocian Greek</a><sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> family of <a href="/wiki/Basil_the_Elder" title="Basil the Elder">Basil the Elder</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Emmelia_of_Caesarea" title="Emmelia of Caesarea">Emmelia of Caesarea</a>, in <a href="/wiki/Cappadocia" title="Cappadocia">Cappadocia</a>, around 330.<sup id="cite_ref-LA336_12-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LA336-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> He was one of ten children, and his parents were known for their piety.<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> His maternal grandfather was a Christian <a href="/wiki/Martyr" title="Martyr">martyr</a>, executed in the years prior to <a href="/wiki/Constantine_I" class="mw-redirect" title="Constantine I">Constantine I</a>'s conversion.<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-R1994-4_15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-R1994-4-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> His pious paternal grandmother, <a href="/wiki/Macrina_the_Elder" title="Macrina the Elder">Macrina</a>, a follower of <a href="/wiki/Gregory_Thaumaturgus" title="Gregory Thaumaturgus">Gregory Thaumaturgus</a> (who had founded the nearby church of <a href="/wiki/Neocaesarea" class="mw-redirect" title="Neocaesarea">Neocaesarea</a>),<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> raised Basil and four of his siblings who also are now venerated as saints: <a href="/wiki/Macrina_the_Younger" title="Macrina the Younger">Macrina the Younger</a>, <a href="/wiki/Naucratius" title="Naucratius">Naucratius</a>, <a href="/wiki/Peter_of_Sebaste" title="Peter of Sebaste">Peter of Sebaste</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Gregory_of_Nyssa" title="Gregory of Nyssa">Gregory of Nyssa</a>.
</p><p>Basil received more formal education in <a href="/wiki/Caesarea_(Mazaca)" title="Caesarea (Mazaca)">Caesarea Mazaca</a> in <a href="/wiki/Cappadocia" title="Cappadocia">Cappadocia</a> (modern <a href="/wiki/Kayseri" title="Kayseri">Kayseri</a>) around 350–51.<sup id="cite_ref-Hildebrand_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hildebrand-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> There he met <a href="/wiki/Gregory_of_Nazianzus" title="Gregory of Nazianzus">Gregory of Nazianzus</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> Gregory went to Alexandria, while Basil went to <a href="/wiki/Constantinople" title="Constantinople">Constantinople</a> for further studies, including the lectures of <a href="/wiki/Libanius" title="Libanius">Libanius</a>. The two later met again in <a href="/wiki/Athens" title="Athens">Athens</a> and became fast friends. There they met a fellow student who would become the emperor <a href="/wiki/Julian_the_Apostate" class="mw-redirect" title="Julian the Apostate">Julian the Apostate</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> Basil left Athens in 356, and after travels in <a href="/wiki/Egypt" title="Egypt">Egypt</a> and <a href="/wiki/Syria" title="Syria">Syria</a>, he returned to <a href="/wiki/Caesarea_(Mazaca)" title="Caesarea (Mazaca)">Caesarea</a>. For around a year he practiced law and taught rhetoric.<sup id="cite_ref-R1994-1_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-R1994-1-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Basil's life changed radically after he encountered <a href="/wiki/Eustathius_of_Sebaste" title="Eustathius of Sebaste">Eustathius of Sebaste</a>, a charismatic bishop and ascetic.<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> Abandoning his legal and teaching career, Basil devoted his life to God. In a letter he described his spiritual awakening:
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>I had wasted much time on follies and spent nearly all of my youth in vain labors, and devotion to the teachings of a wisdom that God had made foolish. Suddenly, I awoke as out of a deep sleep. I beheld the wonderful light of the Gospel truth, and I recognized the nothingness of the wisdom of the princes of this world.<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Annesi">Annesi</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Basil_of_Caesarea&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Annesi">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Basil_of_Caesarea_icon.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6c/Basil_of_Caesarea_icon.jpg/220px-Basil_of_Caesarea_icon.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="314" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6c/Basil_of_Caesarea_icon.jpg/330px-Basil_of_Caesarea_icon.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6c/Basil_of_Caesarea_icon.jpg/440px-Basil_of_Caesarea_icon.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1059" data-file-height="1511" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Basil_of_Caesarea_icon.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Russian icon of Basil of Caesarea</div></div></div>
<p>After his baptism, Basil traveled in 357 to Palestine, Egypt, Syria and Mesopotamia to study ascetics and monasticism.<sup id="cite_ref-Quasten_1986,_p._205_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Quasten_1986,_p._205-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Encyclopædia_Britannica_p._938_25-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Encyclopædia_Britannica_p._938-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> He distributed his fortunes among the poor, and went briefly into solitude near Neocaesarea of Pontus (modern <a href="/wiki/Niksar" title="Niksar">Niksar</a>) on the Iris.<sup id="cite_ref-Quasten_1986,_p._205_24-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Quasten_1986,_p._205-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> Basil eventually realized that while he respected the ascetics' piety and prayerfulness, the solitary life did not call him.<sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Eustathius_of_Sebaste" title="Eustathius of Sebaste">Eustathius of Sebaste</a>, a prominent <a href="/wiki/Anchorite" title="Anchorite">anchorite</a> near Pontus, had mentored Basil. However, they also eventually differed over <a href="/wiki/Dogma" title="Dogma">dogma</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-McSorley_27-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-McSorley-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Basil instead felt drawn toward communal religious life, and by 358 he was gathering around him a group of like-minded <a href="/wiki/Disciple_(Christianity)" title="Disciple (Christianity)">disciples</a>, including his brother Peter. Together they founded a monastic settlement on his family's estate near Annesi<sup id="cite_ref-Encyclopædia_Britannica_p._938_25-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Encyclopædia_Britannica_p._938-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> (modern Sonusa or Uluköy, near the confluence of the <a href="/wiki/Ye%C5%9Fil%C4%B1rmak_River" class="mw-redirect" title="Yeşilırmak River">Iris</a> and <a href="/wiki/Kelkit_River" title="Kelkit River">Lycos</a> rivers<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup>). His widowed mother Emmelia, sister Macrina, and several other women, joined Basil and devoted themselves to pious lives of prayer and charitable works (some claim Macrina founded this community).<sup id="cite_ref-westminster_29-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-westminster-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Here Basil wrote about monastic communal life. His writings became pivotal in developing monastic traditions of the <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Church" class="mw-redirect" title="Eastern Church">Eastern Church</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Attwater_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Attwater-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> In 358, Basil invited his friend Gregory of Nazianzus to join him in Annesi.<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> When Gregory eventually arrived, they collaborated on <i><a href="/wiki/Origen%27s_Philocalia" class="mw-redirect" title="Origen&#39;s Philocalia">Origen's Philocalia</a></i>, a collection of <a href="/wiki/Origen" title="Origen">Origen</a>'s works.<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> Gregory decided to return to his family in Nazianzus.
</p><p>Basil attended the <a href="/wiki/Council_of_Constantinople_(360)" title="Council of Constantinople (360)">Council of Constantinople (360)</a>. He at first sided with Eustathius and the <a href="/wiki/Homoiousian" title="Homoiousian">Homoiousians</a>, a semi-Arian faction who taught that the Son was of <i>like</i> substance with the Father, neither the same (<i>one</i> substance) nor different from him.<sup id="cite_ref-Meredith_1995,_p._22_33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Meredith_1995,_p._22-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> The Homoiousians opposed the Arianism of Eunomius but refused to join with the supporters of the <a href="/wiki/Nicene_Creed" title="Nicene Creed">Nicene Creed</a>, who professed that the members of the Trinity were of one substance ("<a href="/wiki/Homoousios" class="mw-redirect" title="Homoousios">homoousios</a>"). However, Basil's bishop, <a href="/w/index.php?title=Dianius&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Dianius (page does not exist)">Dianius</a> of Caesarea, had subscribed only to the earlier <a href="/wiki/Nicene" class="mw-redirect" title="Nicene">Nicene</a> form of agreement. Basil eventually abandoned the Homoiousians, and emerged instead as a strong supporter of the <a href="/wiki/Nicene_Creed" title="Nicene Creed">Nicene Creed</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Meredith_1995,_p._22_33-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Meredith_1995,_p._22-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Caesarea">Caesarea</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Basil_of_Caesarea&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Caesarea">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:MHS_ojcowie_ks_Bazyli_Wlk_Jan_Chryzostom_Grzeg_Wlk_XVII_Lipie_p.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/82/MHS_ojcowie_ks_Bazyli_Wlk_Jan_Chryzostom_Grzeg_Wlk_XVII_Lipie_p.jpg/220px-MHS_ojcowie_ks_Bazyli_Wlk_Jan_Chryzostom_Grzeg_Wlk_XVII_Lipie_p.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="246" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/82/MHS_ojcowie_ks_Bazyli_Wlk_Jan_Chryzostom_Grzeg_Wlk_XVII_Lipie_p.jpg/330px-MHS_ojcowie_ks_Bazyli_Wlk_Jan_Chryzostom_Grzeg_Wlk_XVII_Lipie_p.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/82/MHS_ojcowie_ks_Bazyli_Wlk_Jan_Chryzostom_Grzeg_Wlk_XVII_Lipie_p.jpg/440px-MHS_ojcowie_ks_Bazyli_Wlk_Jan_Chryzostom_Grzeg_Wlk_XVII_Lipie_p.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2088" data-file-height="2332" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:MHS_ojcowie_ks_Bazyli_Wlk_Jan_Chryzostom_Grzeg_Wlk_XVII_Lipie_p.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Icon" title="Icon">Icon</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Three_Holy_Hierarchs" title="Three Holy Hierarchs">Three Holy Hierarchs</a>: Basil the Great (left), <a href="/wiki/John_Chrysostom" title="John Chrysostom">John Chrysostom</a> (center) and <a href="/wiki/Gregory_the_Theologian" class="mw-redirect" title="Gregory the Theologian">Gregory the Theologian</a> (right)—from Lipie, Historic Museum in <a href="/wiki/Sanok" title="Sanok">Sanok</a>, <a href="/wiki/Poland" title="Poland">Poland</a>.</div></div></div>
<p>In 362, Bishop <a href="/wiki/Meletius_of_Antioch" title="Meletius of Antioch">Meletius of Antioch</a> <a href="/wiki/Ordination" title="Ordination">ordained</a> Basil as a <a href="/wiki/Deacon" title="Deacon">deacon</a>. Eusebius then summoned Basil to Caesarea and ordained him as <a href="/wiki/Presbyter" title="Presbyter">presbyter</a> of the Church there in 365. Ecclesiastical entreaties rather than Basil's desires thus altered his career path.<sup id="cite_ref-Quasten_1986,_p._205_24-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Quasten_1986,_p._205-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Basil and Gregory Nazianzus spent the next few years combating the <a href="/wiki/Arianism" title="Arianism">Arian heresy</a>, which threatened to divide Cappadocia's Christians. In close fraternal cooperation, they agreed to a great rhetorical contest with accomplished Arian theologians and rhetors.<sup id="cite_ref-McGuckin143_34-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-McGuckin143-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> In the subsequent public debates, presided over by agents of <a href="/wiki/Valens" title="Valens">Valens</a>, Gregory and Basil emerged triumphant. This success confirmed for both Gregory and Basil that their futures lay in administration of the Church.<sup id="cite_ref-McGuckin143_34-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-McGuckin143-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> Basil next took on functional administration of the city of Caesarea.<sup id="cite_ref-Attwater_30-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Attwater-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> Eusebius is reported as becoming jealous of the reputation and influence which Basil quickly developed, and allowed Basil to return to his earlier solitude. Later, however, Gregory persuaded Basil to return. Basil did so, and became the administrator for the Diocese of Caesarea.<sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 370, Eusebius died, and Basil was chosen to succeed him, and was consecrated bishop on 14 June 370.<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> His new post as bishop of Caesarea also gave him the powers of <a href="/wiki/Exarch" title="Exarch">exarch</a> of Pontus and <a href="/wiki/Metropolitan_bishop" title="Metropolitan bishop">metropolitan</a> of five <a href="/wiki/Suffragan_bishop" title="Suffragan bishop">suffragan bishops</a>, many of whom had opposed him in the election for Eusebius's successor. It was then that his great powers were called into action. Hot-blooded and somewhat imperious, Basil was also generous and sympathetic. He personally organized a <a href="/wiki/Soup_kitchen" title="Soup kitchen">soup kitchen</a> and distributed food to the poor during a famine following a drought.<sup id="cite_ref-McSorley_27-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-McSorley-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>His letters show that he actively worked to reform thieves and prostitutes. They also show him encouraging his clergy not to be tempted by wealth or the comparatively easy life of a priest, and that he personally took care in selecting worthy candidates for <a href="/wiki/Holy_orders" title="Holy orders">holy orders</a>. He also had the courage to criticize public officials who failed in their duty of administering justice. At the same time, he preached every morning and evening in his own church to large congregations. In addition to all the above, he built a large complex just outside Caesarea, called the Basiliad,<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> which included a poorhouse, hospice, and hospital, and was compared by Gregory of Nazianzus to the <a href="/wiki/Seven_Wonders_of_the_Ancient_World" title="Seven Wonders of the Ancient World">wonders of the world</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>His zeal for <a href="/wiki/Orthodoxy" title="Orthodoxy">orthodoxy</a> did not blind him to what was good in an opponent; and for the sake of peace and charity he was content to waive the use of orthodox terminology when it could be surrendered without a sacrifice of truth. The Emperor <a href="/wiki/Valens" title="Valens">Valens</a>, who was an adherent of the Arian philosophy, sent his <a href="/wiki/Prefect" title="Prefect">prefect</a> Modestus to at least agree to a compromise with the Arian faction. Basil's adamant negative response prompted Modestus to say that no one had ever spoken to him in that way before. Basil replied, "Perhaps you have never yet had to deal with a bishop." Modestus reported back to Valens that he believed nothing short of violence would avail against Basil. Valens was apparently unwilling to engage in violence. He did however issue orders banishing Basil repeatedly, none of which succeeded. Valens came himself to attend when Basil celebrated the <a href="/wiki/Divine_Liturgy" title="Divine Liturgy">Divine Liturgy</a> on the <a href="/wiki/Epiphany_(Christian)" class="mw-redirect" title="Epiphany (Christian)">Feast of the Theophany</a> (Epiphany), and at that time was so impressed by Basil that he donated to him some land for the building of the Basiliad. This interaction helped to define the limits of governmental power over the church.<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Basil then had to face the growing spread of <a href="/wiki/Arianism" title="Arianism">Arianism</a>. This belief system, which denied that Christ was <a href="/wiki/Consubstantiality" title="Consubstantiality">consubstantial</a> with the Father, was quickly gaining adherents and was seen by many, particularly those in Alexandria most familiar with it, as posing a threat to the unity of the church.<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> Basil entered into connections with the West, and with the help of <a href="/wiki/Athanasius" class="mw-redirect" title="Athanasius">Athanasius</a>, he tried to overcome its distrustful attitude toward the Homoiousians. The difficulties had been enhanced by bringing in the question as to the essence of the <a href="/wiki/Holy_Spirit" title="Holy Spirit">Holy Spirit</a>. Although Basil advocated objectively the <a href="/wiki/Consubstantiality" title="Consubstantiality">consubstantiality</a> of the Holy Spirit with the <a href="/wiki/God_the_Father" title="God the Father">Father</a> and the <a href="/wiki/God_the_Son" title="God the Son">Son</a>, he belonged to those, who, faithful to Eastern tradition, would not allow the predicate <i>homoousios</i> to the former; for this he was reproached as early as 371 by the Orthodox zealots among the monks, and <a href="/wiki/Athanasius" class="mw-redirect" title="Athanasius">Athanasius</a> defended him.
</p><p>Basil corresponded with <a href="/wiki/Pope_Damasus_I" title="Pope Damasus I">Pope Damasus</a> in the hope of having his 
aid and encouragement against triumphant Arianism; the pope, however, cherished some degree of suspicion against the Cappadocian Doctor.<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Death_and_legacy">Death and legacy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Basil_of_Caesarea&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Death and legacy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Basil died before the factional disturbances ended. He suffered from liver disease; excessive <a href="/wiki/Ascetic" class="mw-redirect" title="Ascetic">ascetic</a> practices also contributed to his early demise. Historians disagree about the exact date Basil died.<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> The great institute before the gates of Caesarea, the Ptochoptopheion, or "Basileiad", which was used as poorhouse, hospital, and hospice became a lasting monument of Basil's episcopal care for the poor.<sup id="cite_ref-McSorley_27-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-McSorley-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> Many of St. Basil's writings and sermons, specifically on the topics of money and possessions, continue to influence modern Christianity.<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Writings">Writings</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Basil_of_Caesarea&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Writings">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Meister_der_Sophien-Kathedrale_von_Ohrid_001.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fe/Meister_der_Sophien-Kathedrale_von_Ohrid_001.jpg/220px-Meister_der_Sophien-Kathedrale_von_Ohrid_001.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="282" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fe/Meister_der_Sophien-Kathedrale_von_Ohrid_001.jpg/330px-Meister_der_Sophien-Kathedrale_von_Ohrid_001.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fe/Meister_der_Sophien-Kathedrale_von_Ohrid_001.jpg/440px-Meister_der_Sophien-Kathedrale_von_Ohrid_001.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1576" data-file-height="2020" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Meister_der_Sophien-Kathedrale_von_Ohrid_001.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Fresco" title="Fresco">Fresco</a> of Basil the Great in the cathedral of <a href="/wiki/Ohrid" title="Ohrid">Ohrid</a>. The saint is shown <a href="/wiki/Consecration" title="Consecration">consecrating</a> the <a href="/wiki/Sacred_Mysteries" class="mw-redirect" title="Sacred Mysteries">Gifts</a> during the <a href="/wiki/Divine_Liturgy" title="Divine Liturgy">Divine Liturgy</a> which bears his name.</div></div></div>
<p>The principal theological writings of Basil are his <i>On the Holy Spirit</i>, an appeal to Scripture and early Christian tradition to prove the divinity of the Holy Spirit, and his <i>Refutation of the Apology of the Impious Eunomius</i>, which was written about in 364 and comprised three books against <a href="/wiki/Eunomius_of_Cyzicus" title="Eunomius of Cyzicus">Eunomius of Cyzicus</a>, the chief exponent of <a href="/wiki/Anomoeanism" title="Anomoeanism">Anomoian Arianism</a>. The first three books of the <i>Refutation</i> are his work; his authorship of the fourth and fifth books is generally considered doubtful.<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Basilii_Magni_Opera.tif" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5c/Basilii_Magni_Opera.tif/lossy-page1-220px-Basilii_Magni_Opera.tif.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="336" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5c/Basilii_Magni_Opera.tif/lossy-page1-330px-Basilii_Magni_Opera.tif.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5c/Basilii_Magni_Opera.tif/lossy-page1-440px-Basilii_Magni_Opera.tif.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3684" data-file-height="5632" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Basilii_Magni_Opera.tif" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><i>Basilii Magni Opera</i> (1523)</div></div></div>
<p>He was a famous preacher, and many of his homilies, including a series of <a href="/wiki/Great_Lent" title="Great Lent">Lenten</a> lectures on the <i><a href="/wiki/Hexameron" title="Hexameron">Hexaëmeron</a></i> (also <i>Hexaëmeros</i>, "Six Days of Creation"; <a href="/wiki/Latin_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Latin language">Latin</a>: <i lang="la">Hexameron</i>), and an exposition of the <a href="/wiki/Psalter" title="Psalter">psalter</a>, have been preserved. Some, like that against usury and that on the famine in 368, are valuable for the history of morals; others illustrate the honor paid to martyrs and <a href="/wiki/Relic" title="Relic">relics</a>; the <a href="/wiki/Address_to_Young_Men_on_Greek_Literature" title="Address to Young Men on Greek Literature">address to young men on the study</a> of <a href="/wiki/Classical_literature" class="mw-redirect" title="Classical literature">classical literature</a> shows that Basil was influenced by his own education, which taught him to appreciate the <a href="/wiki/Propaedeutics" title="Propaedeutics">propaedeutic</a> importance of the classics.<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> In one of his Homilies, he describes, in one of the earliest descriptions of <a href="/wiki/Desalination" title="Desalination">desalination</a>, how sailors in his time obtain fresh water by hanging sponges over a vessel with boiling seawater and collecting and condensing the water vapor.<sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In his <a href="/wiki/Exegesis" title="Exegesis">exegesis</a> Basil was an admirer of <a href="/wiki/Origen" title="Origen">Origen</a> and the need for the spiritual interpretation of Scripture. In his work on the <a href="/wiki/Holy_Spirit" title="Holy Spirit">Holy Spirit</a>, he asserts that "to take the literal sense and stop there, is to have the heart covered by the veil of Jewish literalism. Lamps are useless when the sun is shining." He frequently stresses the need for Reserve in doctrinal and sacramental matters. At the same time he was against the wild allegories of some contemporaries. Concerning this, he wrote:
</p>
<blockquote><p>"I know the laws of allegory, though less by myself than from the works of others. There are those, truly, who do not admit the common sense of the Scriptures, for whom water is not water, but some other nature, who see in a plant, in a fish, what their fancy wishes, who change the nature of reptiles and of wild beasts to suit their allegories, like the interpreters of dreams who explain visions in sleep to make them serve their own end."<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>His <a href="/wiki/Ascetic" class="mw-redirect" title="Ascetic">ascetic</a> tendencies are exhibited in the <i>Moralia</i> and <i>Asketika</i> (sometimes mistranslated as <a href="https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Saint_Basil:_Ascetical_Works" class="extiw" title="s:Saint Basil: Ascetical Works"><i>Rules</i> of St. Basil</a>), ethical manuals for use in the world and the cloister, respectively. There has been a good deal of discussion concerning the authenticity of the two works known as the <i>Greater Asketikon</i> and the <i>Lesser Asketikon</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-McSorley_27-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-McSorley-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In his book <i>De Spiritu Sancto</i>, Basil lists some who for him are illustrious men of the church and quotes them; these are <a href="/wiki/Irenaeus_of_Lyon" class="mw-redirect" title="Irenaeus of Lyon">Irenaeus of Lyon</a>, <a href="/wiki/Pope_Clement_I" class="mw-redirect" title="Pope Clement I">Clement of Rome</a>, <a href="/wiki/Dionysius_of_Rome" class="mw-redirect" title="Dionysius of Rome">Dionysius of Rome</a>, <a href="/wiki/Dionysius_of_Alexandria" class="mw-redirect" title="Dionysius of Alexandria">Dionysius of Alexandria</a>, <a href="/wiki/Eusebius" title="Eusebius">Eusebius</a>, <a href="/wiki/Sextus_Julius_Africanus" title="Sextus Julius Africanus">Julius Africanus</a>, <a href="/wiki/Gregory_Thaumaturgus" title="Gregory Thaumaturgus">Gregory Thaumaturgus</a>, <a href="/wiki/Firmilian" title="Firmilian">Firmilian</a> and <a href="/wiki/Origen" title="Origen">Origen</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Basil also stresses the complete equality of both genders, deriving from the same human 'lump' (φύραμα‎), both in the image of God, endowed with the same honour and dignity (ὁμοτίμως‎), in perfect equality (ἐξ ἴσου‎). Men even risk being inferior in piety (Homily on Julitta 241B). Likewise, in Homilies on Psalms 1, PG 29.216–17, he insists on the Socratic and Stoic tenet, here Christianized, that man and woman have 'one and the same virtue' and 'one and the same nature' (φύσις‎). Their common creation was of equal honour and dignity (ὁμότιμος‎); they have the same capacity and activity (ἐνέργεια‎), and will be given the same reward. Similarly, in Letter to Amphilochius 188, Basil again uses ἐξ ἴσου‎ in reference to the equality of women and men.<sup id="cite_ref-Ilaria_L.E._Ramelli_2016_49-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ilaria_L.E._Ramelli_2016-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Basil faced the slavery issue in <i>De Spiritu Sancto 20</i> in the context of a Trinitarian debate. Basil recognizes that no human is "a slave by nature". This principle countered <a href="/wiki/Aristotle" title="Aristotle">Aristotle</a>'s conviction and was consistent with <a href="/wiki/Gregory_of_Nyssa" title="Gregory of Nyssa">Gregory of Nyssa</a> view and with that of many other patristic thinkers; even <a href="/wiki/Augustine_of_Hippo" title="Augustine of Hippo">Augustine</a> and <a href="/wiki/Theodoret" title="Theodoret">Theodoret</a> conceded this. Basil, indeed, viewed slavery as a result of the Fall, a principle that was shared by Augustine, Theodoret, and many other Fathers.<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> Sometimes slavery is a boon to the enslaved person, Basil maintained (in <i>Moral Rules 75</i> he recommended, that Christian slaves work harder than non-Christian slaves). This view is opposed to Gregory of Nyssa, and is reminiscent of Aristotle's argument and of Theodoret's.<sup id="cite_ref-Ilaria_L.E._Ramelli_2016_49-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ilaria_L.E._Ramelli_2016-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>It is in the ethical manuals and moral sermons that the practical aspects of his theoretical theology are illustrated. So, for example, it is in his <i>Sermon to the Lazicans</i> that we find Basil explaining how it is our common nature that obliges us to treat our neighbor's natural needs (e.g., hunger, thirst) as our own, even though he is a separate individual.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (December 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>Basil's three hundred letters reveal his character. They show his observant nature, which, despite the troubles of ill-health and ecclesiastical unrest, remained optimistic, tender and even playful. Basil is honoured as the chief architect of monastic life in the Greek Church.<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Most of his extant works, and a few spuriously attributed to him, are available in the <i><a href="/wiki/Patrologia_Graeca" title="Patrologia Graeca">Patrologia Graeca</a></i>, which includes Latin translations of varying quality. Several of Basil's works have appeared in the late twentieth century in the <i><a href="/wiki/Sources_Chr%C3%A9tiennes" title="Sources Chrétiennes">Sources Chrétiennes</a></i> collection.<sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>A famous quote of Basil is the below:<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup>
The bread you store belongs to the hungry. The clothes you accumulate belong to the naked. The shoes that you have in your closet are for the barefoot. The money you bury deep into the ground to keep it safe, belongs to the poor. You were unfair to as many people as you could have helped and you did not.
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:StBasilGreat.JPG" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/df/StBasilGreat.JPG/220px-StBasilGreat.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="293" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/df/StBasilGreat.JPG/330px-StBasilGreat.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/df/StBasilGreat.JPG/440px-StBasilGreat.JPG 2x" data-file-width="2448" data-file-height="3264" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:StBasilGreat.JPG" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Statue of Saint Basil, depicting him in Western <a href="/wiki/Vestment" title="Vestment">vestments</a>, in the Church of St. Nicholas, <a href="/wiki/Mal%C3%A1_Strana" title="Malá Strana">Malá Strana</a>, <a href="/wiki/Prague" title="Prague">Prague</a>, <a href="/wiki/Czech_Republic" title="Czech Republic">Czech Republic</a>.</div></div></div>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Liturgical_contributions">Liturgical contributions</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Basil_of_Caesarea&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Liturgical contributions">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Basil of Caesarea holds a very important place in the history of <a href="/wiki/Christian_liturgy" title="Christian liturgy">Christian liturgy</a>, coming as he did at the end of the age of <a href="/wiki/Persecution" title="Persecution">persecution</a>. That St. Basil composed a liturgy, or rather reformed an existing liturgy, is beyond doubt, since besides the constant tradition of the Byzantine Church there are many testimonies in ancient writings to establish the fact. Basil's liturgical influence is well attested in early sources.<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceA_55-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceA-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Most of the <a href="/wiki/Liturgy" title="Liturgy">liturgies</a> bearing the name of Basil are not entirely his work in their present form, but they nevertheless preserve a recollection of Basil's activity in this field in formularizing liturgical prayers and promoting church-song. Patristics scholars conclude that the Liturgy of Saint Basil "bears, unmistakably, the personal hand, pen, mind and heart of St. Basil the Great".<sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>One liturgy that can be attributed to him is <i><a href="/wiki/Divine_Liturgy_of_Saint_Basil" class="mw-redirect" title="Divine Liturgy of Saint Basil">The Divine Liturgy of Saint Basil the Great</a></i>, a liturgy that is somewhat longer than the more commonly used <i><a href="/wiki/Divine_Liturgy_of_St._John_Chrysostom" class="mw-redirect" title="Divine Liturgy of St. John Chrysostom">Divine Liturgy of St. John Chrysostom</a></i>. The difference between the two is primarily in the silent prayers said by the priest, and in the use of the hymn to the <a href="/wiki/Theotokos" title="Theotokos">Theotokos</a>, <i>All of Creation</i>, instead of the <i><a href="/wiki/Axion_Estin" title="Axion Estin">Axion Estin</a></i> of John Chrysostom's Liturgy.<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceA_55-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceA-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Eastern Churches preserve numerous other prayers attributed to Basil, including three prayers of <a href="/wiki/Exorcism" title="Exorcism">exorcism</a>, several morning and evening prayers, and the "Prayer of the Hours" which is read at each service of the <a href="/wiki/Daily_Office" class="mw-redirect" title="Daily Office">Daily Office</a>.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (December 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Influence_on_monasticism">Influence on monasticism</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Basil_of_Caesarea&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Influence on monasticism">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Christian_monasticism#Byzantine_monasticism" title="Eastern Christian monasticism">Eastern Christian monasticism §&#160;Byzantine monasticism</a></div>
<p>Through his examples and teachings, Basil effected a noteworthy moderation in the austere practices which were previously characteristic of monastic life.<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> He is also credited with coordinating the duties of work and prayer to ensure a proper balance between the two.<sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Basil is remembered as one of the most influential figures in the development of <a href="/wiki/Christian_monasticism" title="Christian monasticism">Christian monasticism</a>. Not only is Basil recognized as the father of Eastern monasticism; historians recognize that his legacy extends also to the Western church, largely due to his influence on <a href="/wiki/Saint_Benedict" class="mw-redirect" title="Saint Benedict">Saint Benedict</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> Patristic scholars such as Meredith assert that Benedict himself recognized this when he wrote in the epilogue to his <i>Rule</i> that his monks, in addition to the Bible, should read "the confessions of the Fathers and their institutes and their lives and the <i>Rule of our Holy Father, Basil.</i><sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> Basil's teachings on monasticism, as encoded in works such as his <i>Small Asketikon</i>, was transmitted to the west via <a href="/wiki/Tyrannius_Rufinus" title="Tyrannius Rufinus">Rufinus</a> during the late 4th century.<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As a result of Basil's influence, numerous religious orders in Eastern Christianity bear his name. In the Roman Catholic Church, the Basilian Fathers, also known as the <a href="/wiki/Congregation_of_St._Basil" title="Congregation of St. Basil">Congregation of St. Basil</a>, an international order of priests and students studying for the priesthood, is named after him.<sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Commemorations">Commemorations</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Basil_of_Caesarea&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Commemorations">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Basil was given the title <a href="/wiki/Doctor_of_the_Church" title="Doctor of the Church">Doctor of the Church</a> in the Western Church for his contributions to the debate initiated by the Arian controversy regarding the nature of the Trinity, and especially the question of the divinity of the <a href="/wiki/Holy_Spirit" title="Holy Spirit">Holy Spirit</a>. Basil was responsible for defining the terms <i>"<a href="/wiki/Ousia" title="Ousia">ousia</a>"</i> (essence/substance) and <i>"<a href="/wiki/Hypostasis_(philosophy_and_religion)" title="Hypostasis (philosophy and religion)">hypostasis</a>"</i> (person/reality), and for defining the classic formulation of three Persons in one Nature. His single greatest contribution was his insistence on the divinity and consubstantiality of the Holy Spirit with the Father and the Son.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETan2013_63-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETan2013-63">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In <a href="/wiki/Greeks" title="Greeks">Greek</a> tradition, Basil <a href="/wiki/Christmas_gift-bringers_around_the_world" class="mw-redirect" title="Christmas gift-bringers around the world">brings gifts to children</a> every 1 January (St Basil's Day). It is traditional on St Basil's Day to serve <i><a href="/wiki/Vasilopita" title="Vasilopita">vasilopita</a></i>, a rich bread baked with a coin inside. The tradition is attributed to St. Basil, who when a bishop, wanted to distribute money to the poor and commissioned some women to bake sweetened bread, in which he arranged to place gold coins. Thus the families in cutting the bread were pleasantly surprised to find the coins.<sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>It is customary on his feast day to visit the homes of friends and relatives, to sing <a href="/wiki/New_Year%27s_Day" title="New Year&#39;s Day">New Year's</a> <a href="/wiki/Category:New_Year_songs" title="Category:New Year songs">carols</a>, and to set an extra place at the table for Saint Basil. Basil, being born into a wealthy family, gave away all his possessions to the poor, the underprivileged, those in need, and children.<sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to some sources, Basil died on 1 January, and the <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church" title="Eastern Orthodox Church">Eastern Orthodox Church</a> celebrates his <a href="/wiki/Feast_day" class="mw-redirect" title="Feast day">feast day</a> together with that of the <a href="/wiki/Feast_of_the_Circumcision" class="mw-redirect" title="Feast of the Circumcision">Feast of the Circumcision</a> on that day. This was also the day on which the <a href="/wiki/General_Roman_Calendar" title="General Roman Calendar">General Roman Calendar</a> celebrated it at first; but in the 13th-century it was moved to 14 June, a date believed to be that of his <a href="/wiki/Ordination" title="Ordination">ordination</a> as <a href="/wiki/Bishop" title="Bishop">bishop</a>, and it remained on that date until the <a href="/wiki/Mysterii_Paschalis" title="Mysterii Paschalis">1969 revision</a> of the calendar, which moved it to 2 January, rather than 1 January, because the latter date is occupied by the <a href="/wiki/Solemnity_of_Mary,_Mother_of_God" title="Solemnity of Mary, Mother of God">Solemnity of Mary, Mother of God</a>. On 2 January Saint Basil is celebrated together with <a href="/wiki/Gregory_Nazianzen" class="mw-redirect" title="Gregory Nazianzen">Saint Gregory Nazianzen</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> Novus Ordo services use the revised calendar while <a href="/wiki/Traditionalist_Catholic" class="mw-redirect" title="Traditionalist Catholic">traditionalist Catholic</a> communities continue to observe pre-1970 calendars.<sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Lutheran_Church%E2%80%93Missouri_Synod" title="Lutheran Church–Missouri Synod">Lutheran Church–Missouri Synod</a> commemorates Basil, along with <a href="/wiki/Gregory_of_Nazianzus" title="Gregory of Nazianzus">Gregory of Nazianzus</a> and <a href="/wiki/Gregory_of_Nyssa" title="Gregory of Nyssa">Gregory of Nyssa</a> on 10 January.<sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Church_of_England" title="Church of England">Church of England</a> celebrates Saint Basil's feast (<a href="/wiki/Lesser_Festival_(Anglicanism)" title="Lesser Festival (Anglicanism)">Lesser Festival</a>) on 2 January, but the <a href="/wiki/Episcopal_Church_(United_States)" title="Episcopal Church (United States)">Episcopal Church</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Anglican_Church_of_Canada" title="Anglican Church of Canada">Anglican Church of Canada</a> celebrate it on 14 June.<sup id="cite_ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the <a href="/wiki/Byzantine_Rite" title="Byzantine Rite">Byzantine Rite</a>, 30 January is the <a href="/wiki/Synaxis" title="Synaxis">Synaxis</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Three_Holy_Hierarchs" title="Three Holy Hierarchs">Three Holy Hierarchs</a>, in honor of Saint Basil, <a href="/wiki/Gregory_Nazianzen" class="mw-redirect" title="Gregory Nazianzen">Saint Gregory the Theologian</a> and <a href="/wiki/John_Chrysostom" title="John Chrysostom">Saint John Chrysostom</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Coptic_Orthodox_Church" title="Coptic Orthodox Church">Coptic Orthodox Church</a> of <a href="/wiki/Alexandria" title="Alexandria">Alexandria</a> celebrates the feast day of Saint Basil on the 6th of <a href="/wiki/Month_of_Tobi" class="mw-redirect" title="Month of Tobi">Tobi</a><sup id="cite_ref-73" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-73">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> (6th of Terr on the <a href="/wiki/Ethiopian_calendar" title="Ethiopian calendar">Ethiopian calendar</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Ethiopian_Orthodox_Tewahedo_Church" title="Ethiopian Orthodox Tewahedo Church">Ethiopian Orthodox Tewahedo Church</a>). At present, this corresponds to 14 January 15 January during <a href="/wiki/Leap_year" title="Leap year">leap year</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-74">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>There are numerous <a href="/wiki/Relic" title="Relic">relics</a> of Basil throughout the world. One of the most important is his head, which is preserved to this day at the monastery of the <a href="/wiki/Great_Lavra" title="Great Lavra">Great Lavra</a> on <a href="/wiki/Mount_Athos" title="Mount Athos">Mount Athos</a> in <a href="/wiki/Greece" title="Greece">Greece</a>. The mythical sword <a href="/wiki/Durandal" class="mw-redirect" title="Durandal">Durandal</a> is said to contain some of Basil's blood.<sup id="cite_ref-75" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-75">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="See_also">See also</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Basil_of_Caesarea&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: See also">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Basilian_monk" class="mw-redirect" title="Basilian monk">Basilian monk</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Pseudo-Basil" title="Pseudo-Basil">Pseudo-Basil</a></li></ul>

<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="External_links">External links</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Basil_of_Caesarea&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: External links">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1134653256">.mw-parser-output .side-box{margin:4px 0;box-sizing:border-box;border:1px solid #aaa;font-size:88%;line-height:1.25em;background-color:#f9f9f9;display:flow-root}.mw-parser-output .side-box-abovebelow,.mw-parser-output .side-box-text{padding:0.25em 0.9em}.mw-parser-output .side-box-image{padding:2px 0 2px 0.9em;text-align:center}.mw-parser-output .side-box-imageright{padding:2px 0.9em 2px 0;text-align:center}@media(min-width:500px){.mw-parser-output .side-box-flex{display:flex;align-items:center}.mw-parser-output .side-box-text{flex:1}}@media(min-width:720px){.mw-parser-output .side-box{width:238px}.mw-parser-output .side-box-right{clear:right;float:right;margin-left:1em}.mw-parser-output .side-box-left{margin-right:1em}}</style><div class="side-box side-box-right plainlinks sistersitebox"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1126788409"/>
